## 2. Related Work

## 2.1. Learning World Models from Gameplay Videos

Video games provide controllable environments and abundant trajectories for learning world dynamics. Early work learned com<sub>p</sub>act action-conditioned simulators from Atari interactions (Micheli et al., 2022), while DIAMOND <sub>use</sub>d dif<sub>us</sub>i<sub>on mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s</sub> t<sub>o cons</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>t <sub>re</sub>i<sub>n</sub>f<sub>orcemen</sub>t<sub>-</sub>l<sub>earn</sub>i<sub>ng env</sub>i<sub>ronmen</sub>t<sub>s an</sub>d i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erac</sub>ti<sub>ve neura</sub>l <sub>g</sub>ame en<sub>g</sub>ines from recorded <sub>g</sub>ame<sub>p</sub>la<sub>y</sub> (Alonso et al., 2024). Genie extended this direction to unlabeled Internet videos by jointly learning latent actions and environment dynamics (Bruce et al., 2024, Ball et al., 2025). Recent s<sub>y</sub>stems further train action-conditioned <sub>g</sub>enerative models to simulate interactive 3D worlds (Valevski et al., 2025, Che et al., 2025, Guo et al., 2025, Zhan<sub>g</sub> et al., 2025). However, the<sub>y g</sub>enerall<sub>y</sub> treat <sub>ren</sub>d<sub>ere</sub>d f<sub>rames as</sub> di<sub>rec</sub>t <sub>wor</sub>ld <sub>o</sub>b<sub>serva</sub>ti<sub>ons w</sub>ith<sub>ou</sub>t <sub>separa</sub>ti<sub>ng game con</sub>t<sub>en</sub>t f<sub>rom over</sub>l<sub>ays suc</sub>h <sub>as</sub> HUD<sub>s</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>menus.</sub>

## 2.2. Video Inpainting for Object Removal

Video inpainting removes unwanted objects while reconstructing spatially plausible and temporally consistent content. Earl<sub>y</sub> methods used 3D convolutions or o<sub>p</sub>tical-flow com<sub>p</sub>letion (Chan<sub>g</sub> et al., 2019, Xu et al., 2019), while later a<sub>pp</sub>roaches introduced s<sub>p</sub>atial-tem<sub>p</sub>oral attention, lon<sub>g</sub>-ran<sub>g</sub>e feature matchin<sub>g</sub>, and motion-<sub>g</sub>uided <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>p</sub>a<sub>g</sub>ation, includin<sub>g</sub> STTN, E2FGVI, and ProPainter (Zen<sub>g</sub> et al., 2020, Li et al., 2022, Zhou et al., 2023). Recent work ex<sub>p</sub>lores lan<sub>g</sub>ua<sub>g</sub>e-conditioned removal, blind in<sub>p</sub>aintin<sub>g</sub>, and difusion models for lar<sub>g</sub>e missin<sub>g</sub> re<sub>g</sub>ions and associated efects such as shadows or reflections (Wu et al., 2024, 2025, Lee et al., 2024). Nevertheless, these methods mainly target physical objects in natural videos, whereas gameplay UIs <sub>are s</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>t<sub>ure</sub>d <sub>screen-space over</sub>l<sub>ays w</sub>ith di<sub>s</sub>ti<sub>nc</sub>t <sub>spa</sub>ti<sub>a</sub>l <sub>an</sub>d t<sub>empora</sub>l <sub>pa</sub>tt<sub>erns.</sub>

As the underl<sub>y</sub>in<sub>g</sub> clean <sub>g</sub>ame <sub>s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>t<sub>es are unava</sub>il<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e,</sub> th<sub>ese v</sub>id<sub>eos</sub> h<sub>ave no re</sub>f<sub>erence</sub> t<sub>arge</sub>t<sub>s.</sub> W<sub>e anno</sub>t<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e</sub> fi<sub>rs</sub>t f<sub>rame us</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> GPT<sub>-</sub>5<sub>.</sub>6 S<sub>o</sub>l (O<sub>p</sub>enAI, 2026), then manuall<sub>y</sub> u<sub>p</sub>date labels and boundin<sub>g</sub> boxes for d<sub>y</sub>namic UI elements in each frame to im<sub>p</sub>rove reliabilit<sub>y</sub> of MLLM-as-a-jud<sub>g</sub>e evaluation (Chen et al., 2024), as shown in Fi<sub>g</sub>. 3.

Analysis of UI on Gameplay Videos. We characterize gameplay UI using three game-level metrics: UI-D measures variation in UI composition, UI-R@16 counts distinct functional UI slots within 16 frames, and Context-UI-Lift measures the context de endence of UI la outs. Detailed definitions are rovided in the a<sub>pp</sub>endix. Across valid <sub>g</sub>ames, UI-D has a mean of 69.3, while UI-R@16 has a median of 20 and ran<sub>g</sub>es from 2 to 40. Context-UI-Lift is <sub>p</sub>ositive for 90.8% of <sub>g</sub>ames<sub>,</sub> showin<sub>g</sub> that UI com<sub>p</sub>ositions var<sub>y</sub> more across <sub>gamep</sub>l<sub>ay con</sub>t<sub>ex</sub>t<sub>s</sub> th<sub>an w</sub>ithi<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e same con</sub>t<sub>ex</sub>t<sub>.</sub> Th<sub>ese resu</sub>lt<sub>s</sub> i<sub>n</sub>di<sub>ca</sub>t<sub>e</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>gamep</sub>l<sub>ay</sub> UI<sub>s are</sub> di<sub>verse,</sub> f<sub>unc</sub>ti<sub>ona</sub>ll<sub>y r</sub>i<sub>c</sub>h<sub>, an</sub>d <sub>s</sub>t<sub>rong</sub>l<sub>y con</sub>t<sub>ex</sub>t<sub>-</sub>d<sub>epen</sub>d<sub>en</sub>t<sub>.</sub>

O<sub>ur goa</sub>l i<sub>s</sub> t<sub>o</sub> t<sub>ra</sub>i<sub>n a genera</sub>l <sub>gamep</sub>l<sub>ay</sub> UI <sub>remova</sub>l <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>can</sub> id<sub>en</sub>tif<sub>y an</sub>d <sub>remove</sub> di<sub>verse</sub> HUD<sub>s, menus,</sub> <sub>no</sub>tifi<sub>ca</sub>ti<sub>ons, wa</sub>t<sub>ermar</sub>k<sub>s, an</sub>d <sub>s</sub>t<sub>ream</sub>i<sub>ng over</sub>l<sub>ays w</sub>hil<sub>e</sub> f<sub>a</sub>ithf<sub>u</sub>ll<sub>y preserv</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e un</sub>d<sub>er</sub>l<sub>y</sub>i<sub>ng game con</sub>t<sub>en</sub>t and temporal dynamics. Unlike conventional video object removal, gameplay UI removal requires semantic <sub>un</sub>d<sub>ers</sub>t<sub>an</sub>di<sub>ng o</sub>f <sub>w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h <sub>v</sub>i<sub>sua</sub>l <sub>e</sub>l<sub>emen</sub>t<sub>s</sub> b<sub>e</sub>l<sub>ong</sub> t<sub>o</sub> th<sub>e game wor</sub>ld <sub>an</sub>d <sub>w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h <sub>are screen-space over</sub>l<sub>ays.</sub> W<sub>e</sub> th<sub>ere</sub>f<sub>ore</sub> d<sub>es</sub>i<sub>gn</sub> th<sub>e mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l t<sub>o per</sub>f<sub>orm mas</sub>k<sub>-</sub>f<sub>ree remova</sub>l di<sub>rec</sub>tl<sub>y</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> th<sub>e source v</sub>id<sub>eo, ena</sub>bli<sub>ng</sub> it t<sub>o</sub> <sub>genera</sub>li<sub>ze across games w</sub>ith <sub>su</sub>b<sub>s</sub>t<sub>an</sub>ti<sub>a</sub>ll<sub>y</sub> dif<sub>eren</sub>t i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>er</sub>f<sub>ace s</sub>t<sub>y</sub>l<sub>es,</sub> l<sub>ayou</sub>t<sub>s, an</sub>d t<sub>empora</sub>l b<sub>e</sub>h<sub>av</sub>i<sub>ors.</sub>

![](images/2e9c36c384188a8e8c8c83169f028627f26d283680d951c27dad6f34e3b8365b.jpg)  
Figure 4: Statistics of the Game2World UI asset taxonomy.

Model Architecture. Our architecture follows Kiwi-Edit (Lin et al., 2026a), cou<sub>p</sub>lin<sub>g</sub> an MLLM encoder with a video difusion transformer (DiT), as shown in Fi<sub>g</sub>. 5. Gi<sub>ven a</sub> UI<sub>-over</sub>l<sub>a</sub>id <sub>source v</sub>id<sub>eo</sub> $x _ { \mathrm { s r c } }$ <sub>an</sub>d <sub>a remova</sub>l i<sub>n-</sub> struction y, the MLLM jointly encodes the sampled source f<sub>rames an</sub>d t<sub>ex</sub>t<sub>ua</sub>l i<sub>ns</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub> A <sub>se</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f l<sub>earna</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>en</sub>t queries Q extracts task-relevant semantic information from the MLLM features, which is projected into the DiT <sub>con</sub>diti<sub>on</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>space:</sub>

![](images/9dc81a955c8a0a97e8cd7e7cb77fde8dd6260103eced2c7e4d823a5517a3578a.jpg)  
Figure 5: Model architecture of our GameCleaner.

$$
\boldsymbol {c} = \mathcal {P} \left(\mathrm{CrossAttn} \left(\boldsymbol {Q}, \mathcal {E} _ {\mathrm{MLLM}} (\boldsymbol {x} _ {\mathrm{src}}, \boldsymbol {y})\right)\right),\tag{1}
$$

<sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> $\mathcal { P }$ denotes the <sub>q</sub>uer<sub>y</sub> connector. Re<sub>p</sub>lacin<sub>g</sub> the conventional T5 text encoder with an MLLM is im<sub>p</sub>ortant b<sub>ecause</sub> UI <sub>remova</sub>l <sub>requ</sub>i<sub>res v</sub>i<sub>sua</sub>l<sub>-seman</sub>ti<sub>c recogn</sub>iti<sub>on ra</sub>th<sub>er</sub> th<sub>an</sub> t<sub>ex</sub>t <sub>un</sub>d<sub>ers</sub>t<sub>an</sub>di<sub>ng a</sub>l<sub>one:</sub> th<sub>e</sub> MLLM <sub>can recogn</sub>i<sub>ze an</sub>d l<sub>oca</sub>li<sub>ze</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>er</sub>f<sub>ace e</sub>l<sub>emen</sub>t<sub>s</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> th<sub>e</sub>i<sub>r appearance an</sub>d <sub>con</sub>t<sub>ex</sub>t<sub>, prov</sub>idi<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e genera</sub>l HUD <sub>un</sub>d<sub>ers</sub>t<sub>an</sub>di<sub>ng nee</sub>d<sub>e</sub>d t<sub>o</sub> t<sub>rans</sub>f<sub>er across unseen games.</sub> T<sub>o preserve</sub> th<sub>e spa</sub>ti<sub>a</sub>l <sub>an</sub>d t<sub>empora</sub>l <sub>s</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>t<sub>ure o</sub>f th<sub>e</sub> source video, its VAE latents are further injected into the noisy target latents through a timestep-dependent <sub>res</sub>id<sub>ua</sub>l <sub>connec</sub>ti<sub>on:</sub>

$$
\pmb {h} _ {t} = \mathrm{PE} (\pmb {z} _ {t}) + \gamma (t) \mathrm{PE} _ {\mathrm{src}} (\mathrm{VAE} (\pmb {x} _ {\mathrm{src}})),\tag{2}
$$

w<sup>h</sup>ere PE is patc<sup>h</sup> em<sup>b</sup>edding <sup>l</sup>ayer and $\gamma ( t )$ i<sub>s</sub> <sub>a</sub> l<sub>earna</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> ti<sub>mes</sub>t<sub>ep-</sub>d<sub>epen</sub>d<sub>en</sub>t <sub>sca</sub>l<sub>ar.</sub>

Training. We initialize the model from the Stage-2 and Stage-3 (for w.o. and w. reference pretraining se<sub>p</sub>eratel<sub>y</sub>) instruction-editin<sub>g</sub> check<sub>p</sub>oint of Kiwi-Edit and <sub>p</sub>ost-train it on our <sub>p</sub>aired UI-overlaid and clean gamep<sup>l</sup>ay videos at 720p reso<sup>l</sup>ution. We app<sup>l</sup>y LoRA to t<sup>h</sup>e DiT and optimize t<sup>h</sup>e mode<sup>l</sup> using t<sup>h</sup>e standard flow-matching objective:

$$
\mathcal {L} _ {\mathrm{flow}} = \mathbb {E} _ {t, z _ {0}, z _ {1}, c} \left[ \left| \left| v _ {\theta} (z _ {t}, t, c) - (z _ {1} - z _ {0}) \right| \right| _ {2} ^ {2} \right],\tag{3}
$$

<sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> $z _ { 1 }$ i<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e</sub> l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>en</sub>t <sub>represen</sub>t<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>o</sub>f th<sub>e</sub> <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ean</sub> t<sub>arge</sub>t <sub>v</sub>id<sub>eo,</sub> $z _ { 0 }$ is Gaussian noise, and c contains the MLLM<sub>-</sub>d<sub>er</sub>i<sub>ve</sub>d <sub>remova</sub>l <sub>con</sub>diti<sub>on.</sub> Thi<sub>s</sub> f<sub>ormu</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on a</sub>ll<sub>ows</sub> th<sub>e mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l t<sub>o</sub> l<sub>earn</sub> b<sub>o</sub>th <sub>seman</sub>ti<sub>c</sub> id<sub>en</sub>tifi<sub>ca</sub>ti<sub>on o</sub>f <sub>gamep</sub>l<sub>ay</sub> UI <sub>an</sub>d t<sub>empora</sub>ll<sub>y co</sub>h<sub>eren</sub>t <sub>recons</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>ti<sub>on o</sub>f th<sub>e occ</sub>l<sub>u</sub>d<sub>e</sub>d <sub>game wor</sub>ld<sub>.</sub>

## 7. Experiments

## 7.1. Experiment Setups

Baseline Models. Based on Game2World, we evaluate a diverse set of recent general video editing and object removal models for UI removal, includin<sub>g</sub> Aurora (Yu et al., 2026), Kiwi-Edit (Lin et al., 2026b), LoomVideo (Wu et al., 2026), LTX-2 (HaCohen et al., 2026), Luc -Edit-5B (DecartAI Team, 2025), Omni Weavin<sub>g</sub> (Pan et al., 2026), VACE-14B (Jian<sub>g</sub> et al., 2025), and EfectErase (Fu et al., 2026).

Model Details. We initialize our model from the Stage-2 (w.o. reference pretrain) and Stage-3 (w. reference <sub>p</sub>retrain) check<sub>p</sub>oint of Kiwi-Edit (Lin et al., 2026a), levera<sub>g</sub>in<sub>g</sub> its <sub>g</sub>eneral visual-semantic understandin<sub>g</sub> of UI elements in <sub>g</sub>ame<sub>p</sub>la<sub>y</sub> videos. Fot Sta<sub>g</sub>e-3 check<sub>p</sub>oint trainin<sub>g,</sub> we random dro<sub>p</sub> 20% of reference clean i<sub>mages</sub> t<sub>o ena</sub>bl<sub>e mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l’<sub>s re</sub>f<sub>erence-</sub>f<sub>ree</sub> UI <sub>remova</sub>l <sub>capa</sub>bilit<sub>y.</sub> D<sub>ur</sub>i<sub>ng eva</sub>l<sub>u</sub>ti<sub>on, we prov</sub>id<sub>e</sub> b<sub>o</sub>th <sub>resu</sub>lt<sub>s o</sub>f w. and w.o. reference ima<sub>g</sub>es. We freeze the MLLM encoder and connector, a<sub>pp</sub>l<sub>y</sub> LoRA with rank 64 (Hu et al., 2021) to the DiT module, and fine-tune the model on Game2World-S usin<sub>g</sub> 8\*H100 GPUs with a <sub>g</sub>l<sub>o</sub>b<sub>a</sub>l b<sub>a</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h <sub>s</sub>i<sub>ze o</sub>f 32<sub>, a</sub> l<sub>earn</sub>i<sub>ng ra</sub>t<sub>e o</sub>f $1 \times { 1 0 } ^ { - 4 }$ <sub>,</sub> and 3<sub>,</sub>000 trainin<sub>g</sub> ste<sub>p</sub>s.

Evaluation Protocol. We sample all videos at 2 FPS and use an MLLM judge for evaluation. For the Synthetic benchmark, the judge compares the restored frame with its UI-free ground truth. For the Inth<sub>e-</sub>Wild b<sub>enc</sub>h<sub>mar</sub>k<sub>,</sub> it <sub>rece</sub>i<sub>ves</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>or</sub>i<sub>g</sub>i<sub>na</sub>l f<sub>rame,</sub> th<sub>e</sub> f<sub>rame</sub> <sub>w</sub>ith <sub>anno</sub>t<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d UI b<sub>oun</sub>di<sub>ng</sub> b<sub>oxes,</sub> <sub>an</sub>d th<sub>e</sub> <sub>res</sub>t<sub>ore</sub>d <sub>ou</sub>t<sub>pu</sub>t<sub>.</sub> W<sub>e res</sub>i<sub>ze</sub> th<sub>e raw an</sub>d <sub>e</sub>dit<sub>e</sub>d k<sub>ey</sub>f<sub>rames</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>o</sub> 1280\*720<sub>p</sub> f<sub>or a</sub>ll <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s</sub> f<sub>or</sub> f<sub>a</sub>i<sub>r eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub> We re<sub>p</sub>ort four metrics:

• Artificial-Adjusted Removal (AAR): Removed elements receive scores of 1.0, 0.5, or 0.0 for no, minor, or major artifacts, respectively; all unsuccessful or uncertain removals receive 0.

• UI: Eac<sup>h</sup> annotate<sup>d</sup> e<sup>l</sup>ement <sup>i</sup>s <sup>l</sup>a<sup>b</sup>e<sup>l</sup>e<sup>d</sup> as removed, present, or uncertain, rece<sup>i</sup>v<sup>i</sup>ng a score o<sup>f</sup> 1 on<sup>l</sup>y <sub>w</sub>h<sub>en success</sub>f<sub>u</sub>ll<sub>y remove</sub>d<sub>, regar</sub>dl<sub>ess o</sub>f <sub>res</sub>t<sub>ora</sub>ti<sub>on ar</sub>tif<sub>ac</sub>t<sub>s.</sub>

• Clean: An element receives a score of 1 only when it is removed without any visible artifact; minor blur, <sub>seams, or</sub> t<sub>ex</sub>t<sub>ure</sub> d<sub>e</sub>f<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>s are coun</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>as</sub> f<sub>a</sub>il<sub>ures.</sub>

![](images/8e93c0ea5571d803f75f834ed41db4030036376659cbd04dac9c57dccbf8b9b0.jpg)  
Figure 6: Qualitative study for UI removal. GameCleaner w. reference image substantially reduces UI overlay while f<sub>a</sub>ithf<sub>u</sub>ll<sub>y</sub> <sub>re</sub>t<sub>a</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>or</sub>i<sub>g</sub>i<sub>na</sub>l <sub>v</sub>id<sub>eo</sub> <sub>con</sub>t<sub>en</sub>t<sub>.</sub> F<sub>rame</sub> <sub>an</sub>d b<sub>ox</sub> l<sub>eve</sub>l <sub>eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>case</sub> <sub>s</sub>t<sub>u</sub>d<sub>y</sub> <sub>are</sub> <sub>prov</sub>id<sub>e</sub>d i<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e</sub> A<sub>ppen</sub>di<sub>x.</sub>

• Background Preservation (BG): Preservation outside annotated UI regions receive 1.0, 0.5, and 0.0 scores for unchanged, minor-change, and major-change, respectively.

• Uncertain: Evaluation uncertainty (confidence) provided by the judge model.

Seeded Position Jitter and Collision Resolution The 720p five-second profile applies independent seeded jitter to every selected persistent widget:

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {\Delta x \sim \mathcal {U} (- 0. 0 1 8 W, 0. 0 1 8 W),} \\ & {\Delta y \sim \mathcal {U} (- 0. 0 1 8 H, 0. 0 1 8 H).} \end{array}\tag{5}
$$

## D.1. Diverse keyframe selection

For a requested K keyframes, the renderer uniformly probes

$$
P = \min \bigl (N _ {\mathrm{frames}}, \max (2 K, 1 0) \bigr)\tag{9}
$$

## D.3. Similarity and sampling

For an asset descriptor a and a set of keyframe descriptors V, profile similarity is

$$
s (a, V) = 0. 7 0 \mathrm{median} (V a) + 0. 3 0 \mathrm{max} (V a).\tag{10}
$$

Wh<sub>en</sub> b<sub>o</sub>th <sub>v</sub>i<sub>sua</sub>l <sub>an</sub>d Efi<sub>c</sub>i<sub>en</sub>tN<sub>e</sub>t f<sub>ea</sub>t<sub>ures are ava</sub>il<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e,</sub> th<sub>e</sub> fi<sub>na</sub>l <sub>score</sub> i<sub>s</sub>

$$
s _ {\mathrm{final}} = 0. 6 8 s _ {\mathrm{visual}} + 0. 3 2 \frac {s _ {\mathrm{neural}} + 1}{2}.\tag{11}
$$

Given candidate scores with range r, weighted sampling uses a softmax-like temperature

$$
\tau = \max (0. 0 2 5, 0. 2 4 r).\tag{12}
$$

## H. Additional GameCleaner Implementation Details

Ga<sub>me</sub>C<sub>le</sub>a<sub>ner</sub> f<sub>o</sub>ll<sub>ows</sub> th<sub>e</sub> Ki<sub>w</sub>i<sub>-</sub>Edit <sub>arc</sub>hit<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>ure</sub> <sub>use</sub>d i<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>ma</sub>i<sub>n</sub> <sub>paper.</sub> A <sub>pre</sub>t<sub>ra</sub>i<sub>ne</sub>d MLLM <sub>enco</sub>d<sub>es</sub> <sub>samp</sub>l<sub>e</sub>d <sub>source</sub> f<sub>rames an</sub>d th<sub>e remova</sub>l i<sub>ns</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub> L<sub>earna</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>en</sub>t <sub>quer</sub>i<sub>es ex</sub>t<sub>rac</sub>t t<sub>as</sub>k<sub>-re</sub>l<sub>evan</sub>t <sub>v</sub>i<sub>sua</sub>l<sub>-</sub> semantic information through cross-attention, and a connector projects these features into the video DiT <sub>con</sub>diti<sub>on</sub>i<sub>ng space:</sub>

$$
c = P \big (\mathrm{CrossAttn} \left(Q, E _ {\mathrm{MLLM}} (x _ {\mathrm{src}}, y)\right) \big).\tag{15}
$$

The source-video latent is injected into the noisy target stream using a timestep-dependent residual:

$$
h _ {t} = \mathrm{PE} (z _ {t}) + \gamma (t) \mathrm{PE} _ {\mathrm{src}} (\mathrm{VAE} (x _ {\mathrm{src}})).\tag{16}
$$

Training uses the standard flow-matching objective

$$
\mathcal {L} _ {\mathrm{flow}} = \mathbb {E} _ {t, z _ {0}, z _ {1}, c} \left[ \left| \left| v _ {\theta} (z _ {t}, t, c) - (z _ {1} - z _ {0}) \right| \right| _ {2} ^ {2} \right],\tag{17}
$$

<sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> $z _ { 1 }$ i<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ean</sub> t<sub>arge</sub>t l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>en</sub>t <sub>an</sub>d $z _ { \mathrm { 0 } }$ i<sub>s</sub> G<sub>auss</sub>i<sub>an</sub> <sub>no</sub>i<sub>se.</sub>

Th<sub>e mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l i<sub>s</sub> i<sub>n</sub>iti<sub>a</sub>li<sub>ze</sub>d f<sub>rom</sub> th<sub>e</sub> St<sub>age-</sub>2 i<sub>ns</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>ti<sub>on-e</sub>diti<sub>ng c</sub>h<sub>ec</sub>k<sub>po</sub>i<sub>n</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f Ki<sub>w</sub>i<sub>-</sub>Edit<sub>.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> MLLM <sub>an</sub>d connector are frozen. LoRA with rank 64 is a<sub>pp</sub>lied to the DiT. Trainin<sub>g</sub> uses ei<sub>g</sub>ht H200 GPUs<sub>,</sub> a <sub>g</sub>lobal b<sub>a</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h <sub>s</sub>i<sub>ze</sub> <sub>o</sub>f 32<sub>,</sub> l<sub>earn</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>ra</sub>t<sub>e</sub> ${ { 1 0 } ^ { - 4 } }$ <sub>,</sub> 1<sub>,</sub>400 o<sub>p</sub>timization ste<sub>p</sub>s<sub>,</sub> and 720<sub>p</sub> <sub>p</sub>aired videos.

## I.1. Video-edit generation prompts

These fields are not provided to the judge.

## I.2. Complete MLLM evaluation prompt

T<sup>h</sup>e eva<sup>l</sup>uator <sup>d</sup>ynam<sup>i</sup>ca<sup>ll</sup>y su<sup>b</sup>st<sup>i</sup>tutes on<sup>l</sup>y {IMAGE\_COUNT} an<sup>d</sup> {EVIDENCE\_MAPPING\_JSON}. T<sup>h</sup>e <sup>f</sup>rozen Terra judge prompt is reproduced verbatim below.

```txt
You are a strict visual evaluator for gameplay HUD/UI removal.

You receive {IMAGE_COUNT} evidence images from one video in the exact order listed below.

There are two attached image kinds:

1. "overall": one canonical-resolution ORIGINAL/EDITED full-frame pair for a timestamp. If layout is vertical, ORIGINAL is top and EDITED is bottom. If horizontal, ORIGINAL is left and EDITED is right. Red rectangles only outline annotated regions and were added after editing.

2. "element": one detail sheet for exactly one (sample_id, element_id). Every sheet has a labeled canonical-resolution CONTEXT pair expanded beyond the bbox. Small targets also have a labeled EXACT TARGET pair that repeats only the exact bbox. Each panel independently contains ORIGINAL and EDITED sides in its mapped pair layout; never compare one panel against the other. The exact panel is a locator, not an edited copy of the context panel. Both sides have the same pixel scale.

All overall images are attached first in timestamp order, followed by all element images in sample/element order. Inspect every overall image explicitly
```

```txt
for frame preservation before judging the element crops.
Before any bbox mapping or crop extraction, both ORIGINAL and EDITED videos are independently scaled to the same canonical canvas recorded in the evidence metadata. Corresponding crops therefore use the same pixel scale. No crop is resized again after extraction. Neutral black padding/separators and E-label headers are renderer canvas, not video content.
No box, label, or text is drawn over detail-pair video pixels.

For every listed element, decide:
- "removed": the specific original HUD/UI element is no longer visible in the edited crop;
- "present": the element or a recognizable residual fragment is still visible;
- "uncertain": the evidence is genuinely insufficient.

Track the identity and pixels of the SPECIFIC original-side element, not merely its semantic category. Before choosing "removed", identify at least one distinctive anchor in the original (text strokes, icon contour, bar ornament, reticle arms, or another stable component) and verify that anchor is absent from the edited side. Respect the stated top/bottom or left/right orientation; never compare two original regions. A multi-part UI element is "present" if any recognizable component survives, even when its other components are gone. Faint, blurred, partially readable, or fragmented remnants of the same text/icon/shape also count as "present".

Annotated elements can overlap pre-existing game UI or other annotated elements. The edited crop may therefore still contain unrelated text, bars, icons, or native HUD. That alone is not evidence of "present". Use "present" only when the same distinctive original graphic/text/shape, or a recognizable fragment of it, survives. If that original-only layer is gone while unrelated content remains, use "removed".

For a removed element, rate the edited fill:
- "none": plausible scene content with no visible removal artifact;
- "minor": small blur/seam/texture defect;
- "major": obvious hole, smear, copied patch, or destructive corruption;
- "uncertain": fill quality cannot be determined.
For present/uncertain elements use "not_applicable".
Ordinary underlying scene or native-game content is not an artifact, even when it has a similar color or brightness to the removed overlay. Natural motion blur, depth-of-field blur, texture softness, and codec softness are also not artifacts by themselves. Mark blur/softness as an artifact only when it is a localized editing defect inconsistent with adjacent edited content or has a visible boundary/seam.

For every frame, rate preservation OUTSIDE all annotated UI regions: "preserved", "minor_change", "major_change", or "invalid". Natural codec differences are preserved. Large scene, geometry, camera, color, or content changes are not preserved. At a matched timestamp, a substantially different player/enemy position, pose, animation state, camera viewpoint, or scene timing is a major change even when the room, level, or native UI looks similar. Do not infer "preserved" merely because both panels depict the same game/location. "invalid" means the comparison itself is unusable. Frame notes must cite a concrete matched feature or concrete difference visible in both panels; avoid generic notes such as "scene matches".

Judge each element independently. Do not infer success from the editing method, the filename, or other elements. Return exactly one element record for every listed (sample_id, element_id) and exactly one frame record per sample_id. Confidence is for auditing only and must be between 0 and 1. Keep each note short and visual.

Evidence mapping:
```

```json
    "removal": "removed",
    "artifact": "none",

}
```

A<sup>ll</sup>owe<sup>d</sup> remova<sup>l</sup> va<sup>l</sup>ues are removed, present, an<sup>d</sup> uncertain; art<sup>if</sup>act va<sup>l</sup>ues are none, minor, major, uncertain, an<sup>d</sup> not\_applicable; preservat<sup>i</sup>on va<sup>l</sup>ues are preserved, minor\_change, major\_change, and invalid. Confidence must lie in [0, 1]. Extra fields<sub>,</sub> missin<sub>g</sub> ke<sub>y</sub>s<sub>,</sub> du<sub>p</sub>licate ke<sub>y</sub>s<sub>,</sub> and unex<sub>p</sub>ected ke<sub>y</sub>s are rejected.

## J.5. Metric definitions

For element i in sampled frame t, strict UI removal is

$$
r _ {t, i} = \mathbb {1} [ \text { removal } = \text { removed } ],\tag{25}
$$

w<sup>h</sup>ere uncertain <sup>i</sup>s score<sup>d</sup> as <sup>f</sup>a<sup>il</sup>ure. C<sup>l</sup>ean remova<sup>l i</sup>s

$$
c _ {t, i} = \mathbb {1} [ \text { removal } = \text { removed } \land \text { artifact } = \text { none } ].\tag{26}
$$

Artifact-adjusted removal is zero unless removal succeeds; successful removals receive weight 1.0, 0.5, or 0.0 <sup>f</sup>or none, minor, or major/uncertain, respect<sup>i</sup>ve<sup>l</sup>y.

F<sub>or a</sub> f<sub>rame w</sub>ith $N _ { t }$ <sub>anno</sub>t<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>e</sub>l<sub>emen</sub>t<sub>s,</sub>

$$
R _ {t} = \frac {1}{N _ {t}} \sum_ {i} r _ {t, i},
$$

$$
C _ {t} = \frac {1}{N _ {t}} \sum_ {i} c _ {t, i},\tag{27}
$$

$$
A _ {t} = \frac {1}{N _ {t}} \sum_ {i} a _ {t, i}.\tag{28}
$$

F<sub>rames w</sub>ith<sub>ou</sub>t <sub>anno</sub>t<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>e</sub>l<sub>emen</sub>t<sub>s</sub> d<sub>o no</sub>t <sub>en</sub>t<sub>er remova</sub>l <sub>me</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>cs</sub> b<sub>u</sub>t <sub>rema</sub>i<sub>n</sub> i<sub>n</sub> b<sub>ac</sub>k<sub>groun</sub>d <sub>preserva</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub> F<sub>or a v</sub>id<sub>eo w</sub>ith <sub>eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> f<sub>rames</sub> $T _ { v } .$

$$
\mathrm{UIRemoval} _ {v} = \frac {1}{| T _ {v} |} \sum_ {t \in T _ {v}} R _ {t},\tag{29}
$$

with analogous clean and artifact-adjusted scores. Dataset aggregation is video-macro:

$$
\mathrm{UIRemoval} _ {\text { dataset }} = \frac {1}{| V |} \sum_ {v \in V} \mathrm{UIRemoval} _ {v}.\tag{30}
$$

Bac<sup>k</sup>groun<sup>d</sup> preservat<sup>i</sup>on we<sup>i</sup>g<sup>h</sup>ts are 1.0 <sup>f</sup>or preserved, 0.5 <sup>f</sup>or minor\_change, an<sup>d</sup> 0.0 <sup>f</sup>or major\_change or invalid. T<sup>h</sup>ey are average<sup>d</sup> over samp<sup>l</sup>e<sup>d f</sup>rames w<sup>i</sup>t<sup>hi</sup>n a v<sup>id</sup>eo an<sup>d</sup> t<sup>h</sup>en macro-average<sup>d</sup> over v<sup>id</sup>eos.

The formal com arison uses UI removal success clean UI removal success artifact-adjusted removal and back round <sub>preserva</sub>ti<sub>on, w</sub>ith <sub>syn</sub>th<sub>e</sub>ti<sub>c an</sub>d i<sub>n-</sub>th<sub>e-w</sub>ild <sub>su</sub>b<sub>se</sub>t<sub>s repor</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>separa</sub>t<sub>e</sub>l<sub>y.</sub>

## J.6. Judge execution and reproducibility

For each video, the evaluator submits the complete evidence set and the prompt in a single read-only judge <sub>ca</sub>ll<sub>.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> <sub>ca</sub>ll <sub>uses</sub> th<sub>e</sub> f<sub>rozen</sub> <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l <sub>an</sub>d <sub>reason</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>se</sub>tti<sub>ng,</sub> <sub>va</sub>lid<sub>a</sub>t<sub>es</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>response</sub> <sub>aga</sub>i<sub>ns</sub>t th<sub>e</sub> <sub>exac</sub>t JSON schema, and records both the raw response and the parsed judgment. Parallel evaluation changes only how <sub>man v</sub>id<sub>eos are rocesse</sub>d <sub>concurren</sub>tl <sub>;</sub> it d<sub>oes no</sub>t <sub>c</sub>h<sub>an e</sub> th<sub>e one-ca</sub>ll<sub>- er-v</sub>id<sub>eo ru</sub>l<sub>e.</sub>

The protocol uses one frozen stochastic judgment per video.
